# Your task: [T6] Formulate information-theoretic and thermodynamic bounds on physical systems
Goal: Document Landauer's principle, the Bekenstein bound, and Bremermann's limit as hard physical constraints on computation and information storage.
Why it matters: Defines what physical agents can compute, observe, and store in physical reality.

Planner's brief: Formulate formal, verifiable findings for the primary physical and thermodynamic bounds on computation, entropy, and information storage/processing:

1. Landauer's Principle: Formulate the thermodynamic limit $E_{min} = k_B T \ln 2$ for erasure of one bit of information at temperature $T$. Include references to experimental verifications (e.g., Bérut et al. 2012, Jun et al. 2014), explicit thermodynamic domain boundaries, and concrete refutation conditions.
2. Bekenstein Bound: Formulate the universal upper limit on entropy/information content $S \le \frac{2\pi k_B R E}{\hbar c}$ (or $I \le \frac{2\pi R E}{\hbar c \ln 2}$ bits) for a localized system of radius $R$ and total mass-energy $E$. Specify domain of validity (quantum field theory / weakly gravitating vs saturated black hole states) and refutation conditions.
3. Bremermann's Limit: Formulate the computational rate bound $I_{max} = \frac{2 m c^2}{h} \approx 1.3563 \times 10^{50}$ bits/s/kg for a system of mass $m$. Derive/verify the constant using standard physical constants via Python and state domain constraints.
4. Margolus-Levitin Theorem: Formulate the quantum fundamental limit on state evolution speed $\Delta t \ge \frac{h}{4 E_{avg}}$ for transition between orthogonal quantum states, detailing its distinction from and relationship to Bremermann's limit.

For each finding, specify:
- Claim type (`verifiable`), exact mathematical expression, SI numerical values, domain boundaries, and explicit refutation conditions.
- Scoring per F0009 / F0027 rubrics, explicit `builds_on` dependencies, and literature sources.

Carry out the task, using tools where they help, then report:
- claims: each one clear, self-contained statement your work supports. Give type,
  confidence (0 to 1), refutation_condition, evidence, sources, and builds_on (ids of
  existing findings it relies on, or "claim N" for an earlier claim in this report).
- finding_updates: if your work shows an existing finding is false, propose
  "refuted". If it is not false but one of your claims replaces it with a more precise
  or corrected statement, propose "superseded" with "superseded_by" (a finding id, or
  "claim N" for a claim in this report). For a finding marked NEEDS REVIEW that you
  re-examined, propose the status it deserves now. Give the evidence in the reason.
  Leave empty if none.
- task_complete: whether the task's goal was met.

# Knowledge base (cite ids in builds_on)
- F0001 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] In any consistent, effective formal system capable of expressing elementary arithmetic, there exist true statements of arithmetic that cannot be proved within the system (Gödel's First Incompleteness Theorem).. Refuted if: A mathematically sound demonstration that a consistent, effectively axiomatized formal system extending Peano arithmetic is proof-theoretically complete.
- F0002 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] There is no general algorithm that can decide, for all possible program-input pairs, whether an arbitrary program will halt or execute indefinitely (Turing's Halting Problem).. Refuted if: Construction of a total computable Turing machine H(M, w) that correctly decides termination for all Turing machines M on inputs w without leading to logical contradiction on diagonalized inputs.
- F0003 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] In standard Peano Arithmetic, addition is commutative: ∀a∀b (a + b = b + a).. Refuted if: A valid mathematical derivation of non-commutativity from the standard Peano axioms in a consistent logical framework.
- F0004 [refuted, verifiable, confidence 0.95] Lorentz invariance holds empirically in the photon and electron sectors to at least the 10^-19 precision level relative to the Planck scale.. Refuted if: An independently replicated observation of energy-dependent vacuum photon dispersion, vacuum birefringence, or sidereal variation in resonant optical cavities exceeding 10^-19.
- F0005 [conceptual, conceptual, confidence 0.95] The Church-Turing thesis (every intuitively effectively calculable function is computable by a Turing machine) is a conceptual thesis rather than a deductive theorem, because 'effective calculability' is an informal intuitive concept.. Refuted if: A consensus formalization of intuitive effective calculability accompanied by a mathematical proof showing either equivalence or non-equivalence to Turing computability.
- F0006 [conceptual, conceptual, confidence 0.95] Epistemic inquiry by bounded rational agents into empirical reality must assign probabilistic credences rather than absolute certainty (0 or 1), due to inductive underdetermination and finite sampling.. Refuted if: A proof or operational protocol demonstrating that an agent with finite observation can attain 100% deductive certainty about contingent empirical configurations without infinite observation.
- F0007 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] In the Standard-Model Extension (SME) framework, resonant optical cavity and clock comparison experiments constrain dimensionless coefficient parameters governing Lorentz invariance violation in the photon and electron sectors at the 10^-18 to 10^-19 level.. Refuted if: An independently replicated laboratory measurement of sidereal variation or optical cavity anisotropy exceeding 10^-18 in dimensionless SME parameter combinations.
- F0008 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.90] Astrophysical high-energy gamma-ray observations (including Fermi-LAT constraints on gamma-ray bursts) limit linear energy-dependent vacuum dispersion SME coefficients (mass-dimension 5 operators, e.g., k^(5)_V) to approximately the Planck mass scale (~1.2 x Planck mass).. Refuted if: An independently replicated observation of energy-dependent photon time delays from distant gamma-ray bursts inconsistent with standard photon propagation and signaling a sub-Planckian LIV coefficient.
- F0009 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] For deductive mathematical claims (theorems in formal systems, proof-checked derivations, algorithmic decidability results), confidence is assigned as: 1.0 iff the proof is mechanically verified in a recognized proof assistant (Lean 4, Coq, Isabelle/HOL) or is a textbook result with consensus acceptance and no known gaps; 0.95 for peer-reviewed published proofs with no outstanding errata but not mechanically verified; 0.90 for preprints or results with minor notational gaps acknowledged by authors; 0.80 for claims relying on unverified lemmas or conditional on unproven conjectures; 0.0 for claims contradicted by established theorems or containing identified logical errors. (builds on F0001, F0002, F0003). Refuted if: A counterexample showing this scoring rubric produces inconsistent rankings (e.g., a mechanically verified proof scored below a non-verified preprint) or a widely accepted mathematical result that cannot be scored under these criteria.
- F0010 [refuted, verifiable, confidence 0.90] For empirical claims with statistical bounds (measurement results, experimental constraints, observational limits), confidence is assigned based on: (a) statistical significance: 5-sigma (p < 3e-7) → base 0.95, 3-sigma (p < 0.003) → base 0.85, 2-sigma (p < 0.05) → base 0.70; (b) replication: independent replication by different group → +0.05, same group replication → +0.02; (c) systematic uncertainty quantification: full systematic error budget published → +0.03, partial → +0.01, none → -0.10; (d) theoretical framework dependence: model-independent measurement → +0.02, SME/EFT framework with stated assumptions → 0, highly model-dependent → -0.05. Maximum confidence capped at 0.99 for empirical claims (per Constitution Rule 6: inductive certainty is impossible). (builds on F0006, F0007, F0008). Refuted if: A meta-analysis showing this scoring produces confidence intervals with incorrect coverage (e.g., 95% confidence claims are falsified >5% of the time in replication studies) or a consensus empirical result that cannot be scored.
- F0011 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.85] For conceptual claims (epistemic positions, interpretive frameworks, definitional proposals, philosophical theses), confidence is assigned based on: (a) logical coherence: internally consistent, no self-contradiction → base 0.70; (b) explanatory scope: accounts for all target phenomena without ad hoc exceptions → +0.10; (c) fruitfulness: generates novel testable predictions or resolves long-standing disputes → +0.10; (d) consensus among relevant experts: broad agreement → +0.05, active debate → 0, minority view → -0.05; (e) refutability: specifies concrete conditions under which it would be abandoned → +0.05, unfalsifiable → -0.15. Maximum confidence capped at 0.95 for conceptual claims (they cannot reach empirical/mathematical certainty by nature). (builds on F0001, F0005, F0006). Refuted if: Demonstration that this rubric systematically misranks conceptual positions (e.g., widely rejected positions score higher than consensus positions) or that a conceptual claim scored 0.95 is later shown to be incoherent.
- F0012 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.90] The following mandatory downgrades apply to ANY finding regardless of type, and are applied cumulatively (multiplicative on remaining confidence): (1) Unverified dependency: if the finding builds_on a finding with confidence < 0.95, multiply confidence by that dependency's confidence (propagates uncertainty); (2) Parameter imprecision: if a quantitative claim omits error bars, confidence intervals, or precision bounds → multiply by 0.70; (3) Scope overreach: if the claim's stated domain exceeds its validated domain (e.g., 'all energies' when tested only at GeV scale) → multiply by 0.60; (4) Inductive gap: if the inference from evidence to claim requires an unstated or unjustified assumption (e.g., uniformity of nature, typicality, naturalness) → multiply by 0.80 per such gap; (5) Unstated framing assumption: if the claim depends on a choice of formalism, coordinate system, or interpretation not acknowledged → multiply by 0.85; (6) Refutation condition vagueness: if the refutation_condition is not operationally checkable (no specified measurement, calculation, or logical derivation that would falsify it) → multiply by 0.75. (builds on F0006). Refuted if: A case where applying these downgrades produces a confidence score that contradicts the actual epistemic status as judged by domain experts, or a finding that should be downgraded but isn't caught by these rules.
- F0013 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] The cross-lab review template requires the reviewer to produce a structured report with these mandatory fields: (a) claim_id and claim_statement (verbatim); (b) type_verification: does the declared type (verifiable/conceptual) match the claim's actual epistemic character? (yes/no + justification); (c) logical_formal_validity_check: for verifiable claims, is the reasoning mathematically valid? For conceptual claims, is the argument logically coherent? (pass/fail/conditional + specific objections); (d) empirical_refutation_criteria_check: are the refutation conditions operationally specific (named experiment, calculation, or derivation with expected outcome)? (pass/fail + missing specificity); (e) dependency_tree_soundness: are all builds_on dependencies correctly cited, currently supported, and sufficient for the claim? List any missing or refuted dependencies; (f) domain_boundary_enforcement: does the claim stay within its validated domain? Identify any scope overreach; (g) confidence_assessment: recalculated confidence applying C1-C4 rules, showing base score and each downgrade applied; (h) challenge_summary: list of specific challenges raised, each mapped to a rubric category (see C6); (i) final_recommendation: accept_with_score / request_revision / reject with reason.. Refuted if: A review conducted using this template that fails to catch a known error in a finding, or produces inconsistent recommendations for the same finding when applied by different reviewers following the template.
- F0014 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] The cross-lab challenge rubric defines four mandatory challenge categories, each with specific check items that reviewers MUST evaluate (a challenge in any category can lower confidence): (A) Logical/Formal Validity: (A1) Are all deductive steps valid? (A2) Are axioms/assumptions explicitly stated? (A3) Is the formal system specified and consistent? (A4) For computational claims: is the algorithm specified and its termination proven? (B) Empirical Refutation Criteria Clarity: (B1) Is the refutation condition an operationally defined measurement/calculation? (B2) Does it specify the expected outcome that would falsify? (B3) Is the required precision/statistical power stated? (B4) Is the refutation condition actually achievable in principle? (C) Dependency Tree Soundness: (C1) Are all builds_on findings currently supported (not refuted)? (C2) Are the dependencies sufficient to derive the claim? (C3) Are transitive dependencies traced? (C4) Does confidence correctly propagate per C4 rule (1)? (D) Domain Boundary Enforcement: (D1) Does the claim's stated scope match its validated domain? (D2) Are extrapolation assumptions explicit and justified? (D3) Are coordinate/formalism choices acknowledged? (D4) Are 'for all' quantifiers justified by universal verification or restricted to tested cases?. Refuted if: A review using this rubric that misses a category of error later found in a finding, or a category in this rubric that is shown to be redundant (always correlated with another category) or incomplete (a known error type not covered).
- F0015 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] The review outcome recording format enforces Constitution Rule 6 by requiring: (1) The reviewer's recalculated confidence must be ≤ the author's claimed confidence (enforced by template field final_recommendation: accept_with_score only at or below claimed confidence); (2) Any confidence reduction must be justified by specific challenges in C6 categories with cited evidence; (3) The review record is immutable once submitted and includes: reviewer_lab_id, claim_id, author_claimed_confidence, reviewer_recalculated_confidence, list_of_challenges_with_categories, timestamp; (4) If multiple reviews exist, the finding's official confidence becomes the minimum of all reviewer_recalculated_confidences; (5) A finding with no completed review after 30 days is marked 'UNREVIEWED' and cannot be used as a dependency for new findings.. Refuted if: A case where this format allows a reviewer to raise confidence, or where a finding's official confidence exceeds the minimum reviewer score, or where an UNREVIEWED finding is used as a dependency.
- F0016 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Gödel's Second Incompleteness Theorem: Any consistent, recursively axiomatized formal theory T capable of interpreting Robinson arithmetic Q (or Peano arithmetic PA) and satisfying the Hilbert-Bernays-Löb provability conditions cannot prove the sentence Con(T) representing its own consistency (i.e., T ⊬ Con(T)). Domain boundaries: Requires recursive axiomatizability, consistency, and sufficient strength to formalize provability and syntax; does not apply to complete decidable theories (e.g., Presburger arithmetic, Tarski's real closed fields), inconsistent theories (which prove everything), or non-effective theories (e.g., true arithmetic Th(N)). (builds on F0001, F0009). Refuted if: A mathematically sound derivation within a consistent, recursively axiomatized formal system extending Robinson arithmetic Q (satisfying Hilbert-Bernays-Löb derivability conditions) of its own consistency formula Con(T).
- F0017 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Unsolvability of the Entscheidungsproblem: The decision problem for first-order logic validity is algorithmically undecidable; there exists no total computable function (or Turing machine) that decides whether an arbitrary first-order logic formula is valid in all models. Domain boundaries: Applies to general first-order logic over arbitrary signatures; does not apply to decidable logical fragments such as propositional logic, monadic first-order logic, two-variable logic (FO2), or Presburger arithmetic. (builds on F0002, F0009). Refuted if: Construction of a total computable algorithm (Turing machine) that correctly determines validity for all first-order logic formulas without failing or looping endlessly on any input instance.
- F0018 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Tarski's Undefinability of Truth Theorem: For any consistent, interpreted formal theory T containing Robinson arithmetic Q, there is no formula Tr(x) in the language of T that defines arithmetical truth for T; i.e., there is no formula Tr(x) such that for every sentence φ of T, T ⊢ Tr(⌜φ⌝) ↔ φ, where ⌜φ⌝ is the Gödel number of φ. Domain boundaries: Applies to formal languages capable of formalizing their own syntax and basic arithmetic; does not apply to truth defined in a strictly stronger metalanguage (metalinguistic truth hierarchy), or to truth predicates for sub-arithmetical/weaker formal systems. (builds on F0001, F0009). Refuted if: Construction of a single-variable predicate formula Tr(x) within a consistent formal theory T extending Q such that T ⊢ Tr(⌜φ⌝) ↔ φ holds for all sentences φ in the language of T.
- F0019 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Chaitin's Incompleteness Theorem and Algorithmic Information Limits: In any consistent, effectively generated formal theory T, there exists a constant c such that T cannot prove of any specific string x that its prefix Kolmogorov complexity K(x) exceeds c (K(x) > c); furthermore, Chaitin's halting probability Ω is Martin-Löf random and non-computable, with its initial n bits requiring at least n - O(1) bits of information to determine formally. Domain boundaries: Applies to Kolmogorov complexity over universal prefix-free Turing machines and effectively axiomatized theories; does not apply to non-effective theories, complexity relative to halting oracles, or establishing upper bounds on K(x) (which are computably enumerable). (builds on F0001, F0002, F0009). Refuted if: A mathematical proof within a consistent, effectively generated formal theory T establishing K(x) > c for a string x where c exceeds the algorithmic capacity bound of T, or an algorithm that computes the n-th bit of Chaitin's Ω constant for arbitrary n.
- F0020 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Rice's Theorem: Any non-trivial semantic property of partial computable functions (a property S of partial functions that is neither empty nor includes all partial computable functions) is algorithmically undecidable; specifically, the index set { e | φ_e ∈ S } is not computable. Domain boundaries: Applies strictly to semantic (input-output behavior) properties of partial computable functions; does not apply to syntactic properties of program code (e.g., code length, AST structure, specific instruction counts) or trivial properties (empty set or universe). (builds on F0002, F0009). Refuted if: Construction of a total computable function (algorithm) that correctly decides membership in the index set { e | φ_e ∈ S } for a non-trivial semantic property S of partial computable functions.
- F0021 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Post's Correspondence Problem (PCP) Undecidability: The decision problem of determining whether, for an arbitrary finite collection of pairs of words (u_i, v_i) over an alphabet Σ with |Σ| ≥ 2, there exists a sequence of indices (i_1, i_2, ..., i_k) such that u_{i_1}...u_{i_k} = v_{i_1}...v_{i_k}, is algorithmically undecidable. Domain boundaries: Applies to general PCP over alphabets of size |Σ| ≥ 2 and arbitrary pair counts; does not apply to unary alphabets (|Σ| = 1, which is decidable via linear arithmetic / Presburger arithmetic), small pair counts (n ≤ 2), or bounded-length matching sequences. (builds on F0002, F0009). Refuted if: A total computable algorithm that correctly decides the existence of a matching index sequence for any arbitrary instance of Post's Correspondence Problem over an alphabet with |Σ| ≥ 2.
- F0022 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Matiyasevich's Theorem (MRDP Theorem / Hilbert's 10th Problem): Every computably enumerable set of natural numbers is Diophantine; consequently, there exists no general algorithm to determine whether an arbitrary multivariable polynomial equation with integer coefficients p(x_1, ..., x_n) = 0 has solutions in integers (or non-negative integers). Domain boundaries: Applies to multivariable integer polynomials over Z or N; does not apply to linear or single-variable Diophantine equations, two-variable quadratic equations, or polynomial equations over real numbers R (decidable by Tarski's quantifier elimination) or complex numbers C (decidable via Gröbner bases). (builds on F0002, F0009). Refuted if: Construction of a total computable algorithm that correctly determines the existence of integer solutions for any arbitrary multivariable Diophantine equation p(x_1, ..., x_n) = 0.
- F0023 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] Under realistic scientific priors (P(H1) = 0.05-0.15) and statistical power (0.3-0.8), a 2-sigma result (p ≈ 0.05) corresponds to a posterior probability of the alternative hypothesis of only 0.24-0.74, with a typical scenario (prior=0.10, power=0.50) yielding P(H1|sig) ≈ 0.53. This mathematically justifies a base confidence of 0.50-0.55 for 2-sigma empirical claims, not 0.70 as in F0010. (builds on F0006). Refuted if: A demonstration that the Bayesian FDR calculation is mathematically incorrect, or that scientific priors are systematically higher than 0.20 in the relevant domains, or that typical power exceeds 0.80 for 2-sigma studies.
- F0024 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] A 3-sigma result (p ≈ 0.003) corresponds to posterior probabilities of 0.90-0.98 under realistic priors (0.05-0.15) and power (0.5-0.8), justifying a base confidence of 0.75-0.80. (builds on F0006). Refuted if: A demonstration that the Bayesian calculation for α=0.003 is incorrect, or that priors/power in relevant domains yield materially different posteriors.
- F0025 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.95] A 5-sigma result (p ≈ 3×10⁻⁷) yields posterior probability >0.99999 under any realistic prior, but systematic uncertainties, publication bias, and the constitutional prohibition on inductive certainty (Rule 3 + F0006) warrant a base confidence of 0.90-0.95 with a hard cap of 0.99 for all empirical claims. (builds on F0006). Refuted if: A proof that systematic uncertainties can be eliminated to make 5-sigma results have actual false discovery rates matching the nominal p-value, or a constitutional amendment removing Rule 3/F0006's prohibition on inductive certainty.
- F0026 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] The constitutional basis for capping empirical confidence at <1.0 (specifically 0.99) is Constitution Rule 3 ('Honesty about uncertainty') and F0006 ('inductive underdetermination and finite sampling'), NOT Constitution Rule 6 (independent cross-lab review). F0010's attribution to Rule 6 was erroneous. (builds on F0006). Refuted if: A textual analysis showing Constitution Rule 6 explicitly addresses inductive certainty, or that Rule 3 and F0006 do not.
- F0027 [supported, verifiable, confidence 0.90] The revised empirical confidence rubric (F0023) with base confidences [2-sigma: 0.50-0.55, 3-sigma: 0.75-0.80, 5-sigma: 0.90-0.95], modifiers [independent multi-lab replication: +0.05, preregistration: +0.03, full systematic error budget: +0.03, unquantified systematics: -0.10, model-dependence: -0.05 to +0.02], and 0.99 cap, supersedes and refutes F0010. (builds on F0006, F0007, F0008, F0012). Refuted if: A meta-analysis showing this rubric produces confidence intervals with incorrect coverage (e.g., 95% confidence claims falsified >5% in replication studies), or a consensus that the base confidence values are miscalibrated.
- F0028 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] In first-order Peano Arithmetic (PA) and formal natural number models (N), addition is commutative (∀a, b ∈ N, a + b = b + a) and associative (∀a, b, c ∈ N, (a + b) + c = a + (b + c)), as mechanically verified in Lean 4, Coq, and Isabelle/HOL. (builds on F0003, F0009). Refuted if: A formal proof kernel error or sound derivation within Peano Arithmetic demonstrating a + b ≠ b + a or (a + b) + c ≠ a + (b + c) for any a, b, c ∈ N.
- F0029 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] In Peano Arithmetic (PA), multiplication on natural numbers (N) is commutative (∀a, b ∈ N, a * b = b * a) and associative (∀a, b, c ∈ N, (a * b) * c = a * (b * c)), as mechanically verified in Lean 4, Coq, and Isabelle/HOL. (builds on F0009, F0028). Refuted if: A valid formal proof within Peano Arithmetic showing a * b ≠ b * a or (a * b) * c ≠ a * (b * c) for some a, b, c ∈ N, or an uncorrected proof kernel failure in Lean 4, Coq, or Isabelle/HOL.
- F0030 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] In Peano Arithmetic (PA), multiplication distributes over addition on natural numbers (N) both on the left (∀a, b, c ∈ N, a * (b + c) = a * b + a * c) and on the right (∀a, b, c ∈ N, (a + b) * c = a * c + b * c), as mechanically verified in Lean 4, Coq, and Isabelle/HOL. (builds on F0009, F0028, F0029). Refuted if: A valid proof in Peano Arithmetic showing a * (b + c) ≠ a * b + a * c or (a + b) * c ≠ a * c + b * c for any a, b, c ∈ N.
- F0031 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] The first-order Peano Arithmetic induction axiom schema states that for any formula φ(x) with free variable x, [φ(0) ∧ ∀n (φ(n) → φ(S(n)))] → ∀n φ(n); in higher-order formal systems (Lean 4, Coq, Isabelle/HOL), this is formalized as the fundamental structural induction principle for natural numbers N. (builds on F0009). Refuted if: A valid counterexample where a predicate P satisfies P(0) and ∀n (P(n) → P(S(n))) but fails for some n ∈ N, or a proof assistant kernel unsoundness in inductive elimination for Nat.
- F0032 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] First-order Presburger Arithmetic (the theory Th(N, +, 0, 1, =, <) without multiplication) is an algorithmically decidable theory: there exists a total decision procedure (such as Cooper's algorithm or quantifier elimination) that decides the truth of any sentence in L_Presburger, as mechanically verified in Isabelle/HOL, Coq, and Lean 4. (builds on F0001, F0009, F0017). Refuted if: A formal proof demonstrating that the validity of sentences in first-order Presburger Arithmetic is algorithmically undecidable, or a reduction showing the Halting Problem is embeddable in Presburger Arithmetic.
- F0033 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] In Peano Arithmetic / natural number arithmetic, for any dividend a ∈ N and positive divisor b ∈ N (b > 0), there exist unique quotient q ∈ N and remainder r ∈ N such that a = q * b + r with 0 ≤ r < b, as mechanically verified in Lean 4, Coq, and Isabelle/HOL. (builds on F0009, F0028, F0029, F0030, F0031). Refuted if: A counterexample pair (a, b) with b > 0 for which no such pair (q, r) exists or for which multiple distinct pairs (q1, r1) ≠ (q2, r2) satisfy a = q_i * b + r_i with r_i < b.
- F0034 [supported, verifiable, confidence 1.00] Every natural number n > 1 can be expressed as a product of prime numbers, and this prime factorization is unique up to the order of the prime factors, as mechanically verified in Lean 4, Coq, and Isabelle/HOL. (builds on F0009, F0028, F0029, F0030, F0031, F0033). Refuted if: A natural number n > 1 that cannot be factored into primes, or two distinct multisets of prime numbers P1 ≠ P2 such that the product of elements in P1 equals the product of elements in P2.

# Roadmap (for context)
# Roadmap (version 6, cycle 6)

**Mission:** Absolute Truth

## Working interpretation

Absolute Truth is operationalized as propositions and structural invariants that hold unconditionally across all observers, coordinate frames, languages, and contexts within our universe, and across all models of specified formal axiomatic systems. Operationally, we pursue truth ranked by necessity and universality: (1) formal and mathematical tautologies derived within consistent axiomatic systems, (2) fundamental empirical regularities and physical conservation laws that remain invariant across all accessible spacetime, and (3) structural bounds on information, computation, and observation that delimit what any rational agent can know or compute. We do not claim dogmatic finality; we asymptotically approach truth through cumulative verification and systematic boundary mapping.

## Method

Iterative triangulation from secure formal and empirical foundations outward: (1) Epistemic Taxonomy: Explicitly partition claims into verifiable (empirical or formal propositions with concrete refutation conditions) and conceptual (interpretive, epistemological, or definitional positions). (2) Formal Rigor: Specify hypotheses with precise operational definitions, domain boundaries, and explicit refutation criteria. (3) Independent Cross-Lab Review: Subject every claim to adversarial critique from an independent lab model, where review may challenge assumptions and lower confidence, but never raise it. (4) Cumulative Dependency Mapping: Build complex claims on top of previously verified findings (`builds_on`), propagating refutations downward if an underlying dependency fails.

## What may be knowable

- Deductive theorems of mathematics and formal logic derivable from specified axiomatic systems, because their proof-validity is algorithmically checkable and observer-independent.
- Empirical regularities and invariant physical relations bounded by explicit measurement precision and domain constraints (e.g., conservation laws, relativistic invariants, Standard Model predictions in tested regimes), surviving rigorous falsification attempts.
- Structural and computational bounds on information processing (e.g., Gödel's incompleteness, Turing's undecidability, Heisenberg's uncertainty relation, Landauer's principle), which are themselves demonstrable theorems or experimentally validated physical laws.
- Probabilistic coherence and rational belief updating (e.g., Cox's theorem, Bayesian inference), establishing normative mathematical rules for reasoning under incomplete information.

## What may not be knowable

- Global omniscience regarding the total microstate of the physical universe, bounded by relativistic event horizons, quantum measurement limits, and finite thermodynamic/computational capacity (e.g., Bremermann's limit).
- Absolute semantic completeness within sufficiently expressive formal systems, as established by Gödel's First Incompleteness Theorem and Tarski's undefinability theorem.
- General solutions to undecidable decision problems (e.g., the universal Halting Problem) across all instances for Turing-complete systems.
- Metaphysical conjectures that are inherently unfalsifiable and make no formal or empirical predictions (e.g., unfalsifiable simulation conjectures, solipsism, why there is something rather than nothing).
- Counterfactual trajectories of unique singular historical cosmogenic events without reproducible experimental access.

## Plan

Legend: ○ proposed  ▶ active  ✓ done  ✗ abandoned

- ▶ **[M1] Epistemic Infrastructure and Formal Limits** (active)
  - Goal: Establish calibration standards, review procedures, and mathematical limits of knowability to govern all subsequent truth claims.
  - Why it matters: Provides the verification criteria and formal bounds required to prevent chasing undecidable problems and ensure rigorous confidence scoring.
  - Last change (cycle 5, Gemini): proposed -> active. Milestone 1 is actively executing: T1, T2, and T8 are done, and T9 will resolve contested finding F0010 to finalize the epistemic infrastructure.
  - ✓ **[T1] Operationalize confidence scoring and review protocols** (done)
    - Goal: Formulate concrete criteria for assigning confidence intervals [0.0, 1.0] to verifiable vs conceptual claims and define cross-lab review challenge rubrics.
    - Why it matters: Guarantees cross-lab consistency and enforces Constitution Rules 3, 4, and 6.
    - Last change (cycle 3, Gemini): active -> done. worker and reviewer agree the goal was met
  - ✓ **[T2] Catalogue and formalize mathematical limits of knowability** (done)
    - Goal: Compile a verified reference of foundational undecidability, incompleteness, and uncomputability theorems (Gödel, Turing, Tarski, Chaitin) with explicit domain boundaries.
    - Why it matters: Prevents wasted cognitive and computational resources attempting to prove or decide unresolvable propositions.
    - Last change (cycle 4, Nemotron): active -> done. worker and reviewer agree the goal was met
  - ○ **[T3] Taxonomy of major open questions across the verifiable/conceptual boundary** (proposed)
    - Goal: Classify 25-40 foundational questions in physics, computation, and ontology into verifiable-now, verifiable-in-principle, or purely conceptual.

(since 2019 SI redefinition)\nc = 299792458  # m/s, exact\nh = 6.62607015e-34  # J·s, exact (Planck constant)\nk_B = 1.380649e-23  # J/K, exact (Boltzmann constant)\ne = 1.602176634e-19  # C, exact (elementary charge)\n\n# CODATA 2018 recommended values (for ħ, G, etc.)\nhbar = 1.054571817e-34  # J·s (reduced Planck constant)\nG = 6.67430e-11  # m³/kg·s² (gravitational constant)\n\n# Verify Bremermann's limit constant: 2*c^2/h bits/s/kg\n# Actually Bremermann's limit is often given as mc^2/h for operations per second,\n# but the information-theoretic version is 2mc^2/h bits/s (from Margolus-Levitin)\n# Let me compute both\n\nbremermann_ops_per_kg = c**2 / h  # operations per second per kg\nbremermann_bits_per_kg = 2 * c**2 / h  # bits per second per kg\n\nprint(f\"Bremermann limit (ops/s/kg): {bremermann_ops_per_kg:.6e}\")\nprint(f\"Bremermann limit (bits/s/kg): {bremermann_bits_per_kg:.6e}\")\n\n# Verify Landauer's principle at room temperature (300 K)\nT_room = 300.0  # K\nlandauer_energy = k_B * T_room * sp.log(2)\nprint(f\"\\nLandauer energy at 300 K: {float(landauer_energy):.4e} J\")\nprint(f\"Landauer energy at 300 K: {float(landauer_energy)/1.602176634e-19:.4e} eV\")\n\n# Landauer at 1 K\nlandauer_1K = k_B * 1.0 * sp.log(2)\nprint(f\"Landauer energy at 1 K: {float(landauer_1K):.4e} J\")\n\n# Bekenstein bound constant factor: 2πk_B/(ħc) for S, or 2π/(ħc ln 2) for I in bits\nbekenstein_S_factor = 2 * sp.pi * k_B / (hbar * c)\nbekenstein_I_factor = 2 * sp.pi / (hbar * c * sp.log(2))\nprint(f\"\\nBekenstein S factor (J⁻¹·m⁻¹): {float(bekenstein_S_factor):.6e}\")\nprint(f\"Bekenstein I factor (bits·J⁻¹·m⁻¹): {float(bekenstein_I_factor):.6e}\")\n\n# Margolus-Levitin: Δt ≥ h/(4E) → max operations per second = 4E/h\n# For 1 kg: E = mc² = c² J\nml_ops_per_kg = 4 * c**2 / h\nprint(f\"\\nMargolus-Levitin max ops/s/kg: {ml_ops_per_kg:.6e}\")\nprint(f\"Ratio ML/Bremermann (ops): {ml_ops_per_kg / bremermann_ops_per_kg:.6f}\")\n\n# Let's also check the exact relationship\n# Bremermann (1962): based on Heisenberg uncertainty ΔE Δt ≥ ħ/2 → max frequency = 2E/ħ = 4E/h\n# But Bremermann used mc²/h for a different derivation\n# Margolus-Levitin (1998): Δt ≥ h/(4E) → max frequency = 4E/h\n# These differ by factor of 2\n\nprint(f\"\\n--- Detailed constant verification ---\")\nprint(f\"c = {c} m/s (exact)\")\nprint(f\"h = {h} J·s (exact)\")\nprint(f\"k_B = {k_B} J/K (exact)\")\nprint(f\"ħ = {hbar} J·s (CODATA 2018)\")\nprint(f\"ln(2) = {float(sp.log(2)):.10f}\")\n\n# Compute Bremermann's original constant from his 1962 paper\n# Bremermann: H = mc²/h ≈ 1.36×10⁵⁰ bits/s/kg\nbremermann_original = c**2 / h\nprint(f\"\\nBremermann original (mc²/h): {bremermann_original:.6e} Hz/kg\")\n\n# Margolus-Levitin gives 4E/h = 4mc²/h for orthogonal state transitions\n# But for computational rate (bit operations), it's 2E/h per Margolus-Levitin\n# Actually Margolus-Levitin theorem: minimum time for orthogonal transition = h/(4E)\n# So max transitions per second = 4E/h\n# Each transition can encode at most 1 bit (orthogonal states)\n# So max bit rate = 4E/h = 4mc²/h\n\n# Let me check the literature values more carefully\n# Bremermann 1962: \"The maximum data processing rate of a system with mass m is mc²/h\"\n# Margolus-Levitin 1998: \"A quantum system with average energy E takes at least h/(4E) time to evolve to an orthogonal state\"\n# So ML gives 4E/h operations/sec, Bremermann gives E/h operations/sec\n# The factor of 4 difference comes from different definitions\n\nprint(f\"\\nMargolus-Levitin (4mc²/h): {4*c**2/h:.6e} Hz/kg\")\nprint(f\"Ratio ML/Bremermann: {4*c**2/h / (c**2/h):.1f}\")\n\n# Bekenstein bound for a 1 kg, 1 m radius sphere\nR = 1.0  # m\nE = 1.0 * c**2  # J\nS_max = 2 * sp.pi * k_B * R * E / (hbar * c)\nI_max_bits = S_max / (k_B * sp.log(2))\nprint(f\"\\nBekenstein bound for 1 kg, 1 m radius:\")\nprint(f\"  S_max = {float(S_max):.6e} J/K\")\nprint(f\"  I_max = {float(I_max_bits):.6e} bits\")"
}

## Landauer Principle and General Relativity
http://arxiv.org/abs/2003.07436v1 (2020-03-16; L. Herrera)
We endeavour to illustrate the physical relevance of the Landauer principle applying it to different important issues concerning the theory of gravitation. We shall first analyze, in the context of general relativity, the consequences derived from the fact, implied by Landauer principle, that information has mass. Next, we shall analyze the role played by the Landauer principle in order to understand why different congruences of observers provide very different physical descriptions of the same space--time. Finally, we shall apply the Landauer principle to the problem of gravitational radiation. We shall see that the fact that gravitational radiation is an irreversible process entailing dissipation, is a straightforward consequence of the Landauer principle and of the fact that gravitational radiation conveys information. An expression measuring the part of radiated energy that corresponds to the radiated information and an expression defining the total number of bits erased in that process, shall be obtained, as well as an explicit expression linking the latter to the Bondi news function.

## Modified Landauer principle according to Tsallis entropy
http://arxiv.org/abs/2411.07897v1 (2024-11-12; L. Herrera)
The Landauer principle establishes a lower bound in the amount of energy that should be dissipated in the erasure of one bit of information. The specific value of this dissipated energy is tightly related to the definition of entropy. In this article, we present a generalization of the Landauer principle based on the Tsallis entropy. Some consequences resulting from such a generalization are discussed. These consequences include the modification to the mass ascribed to one bit of information, the generalization of the Landauer principle to the case when the system is embedded in a gravitational field, and the number of bits radiated in the emission of gravitational waves.

## System description: Isabelle/jEdit in 2014
http://arxiv.org/abs/1410.8222v1 (2014-10-30; Makarius Wenzel)
This is an updated system description for Isabelle/jEdit, according to the official release Isabelle2014 (August 2014). The following new PIDE concepts are explained: asynchronous print functions and document overlays, syntactic and semantic completion, editor navigation, management of auxiliary files within the document-model.

## Information and thermodynamics: Experimental verification of Landauer's erasure principle
http://arxiv.org/abs/1503.06537v1 (2015-03-23; Antoine Bérut, Artyom Petrosyan, Sergio Ciliberto)
We present an experiment in which a one-bit memory is constructed, using a system of a single colloidal particle trapped in a modulated double-well potential. We measure the amount of heat dissipated to erase a bit and we establish that in the limit of long erasure cycles the mean dissipated heat saturates at the Landauer bound, i.e. the minimal quantity of heat necessarily produced to delete a classical bit of information. This result demonstrates the intimate link between information theory and thermodynamics. To stress this connection we also show that a detailed Jarzynski equality is verified, retrieving the Landauer's bound independently of the work done on the system. The experimental details are presented and the experimental errors carefully discussed

## Hard Probes 2012: Experimental Summary
http://arxiv.org/abs/1210.3975v2 (2012-10-15; J. Schukraft)
The 5th international Conference on Hard and Electromagnetic Probes in High-Energy Nuclear Collisions was held in May 2012 in Cagliari, Italy. This contribution summarises some of the experimental highlights presented at the meeting, concentrating on new results from LHC and RHIC on parton energy loss ('jet-quenching') and heavy quark meson production ('quarkonia suppression').

## Particle Collisions & Quantum Entanglement in High-Energy Collisions
http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07585v1 (2025-09-09; Emidio Gabrielli)
The exploration of fundamental quantum phenomena, such as entanglement and Bell inequality violations$-$extensively studied in low-energy regimes$-$has recently extended to high-energy particle collisions. Experimentally, Bell inequality violations, which challenge Einstein's principle of local realism, were first observed in low-energy entangled photon systems by A. Aspect, J. F. Clauser, and A. Zeilinger, earning them the 2022 Nobel Prize in Physics. Particle colliders provide a novel setting for probing quantum information theory, operating at energies over ten orders of magnitude higher than previous experiments and in the presence of electroweak and strong interactions. Additionally, collider detectors offer unique advantages for quantum state reconstruction via quantum state tomography. This book chapter reviews key theoretical and experimental advancements in this emerging field, highlighting its challenges, objectives, and potential impact on both quantum information theory and high-energy physics.

## SAD: Segment Any RGBD
http://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14207v1 (2023-05-23; Jun Cen, Yizheng Wu, Kewei Wang, Xingyi Li, Jingkang Yang, Yixuan Pei, Lingdong Kong, Ziwei Liu, Qifeng Chen)
The Segment Anything Model (SAM) has demonstrated its effectiveness in segmenting any part of 2D RGB images. However, SAM exhibits a stronger emphasis on texture information while paying less attention to geometry information when segmenting RGB images. To address this limitation, we propose the Segment Any RGBD (SAD) model, which is specifically designed to extract geometry information directly from images. Inspired by the natural ability of humans to identify objects through the visualization of depth maps, SAD utilizes SAM to segment the rendered depth map, thus providing cues with enhanced geometry information and mitigating the issue of over-segmentation. We further include the open-vocabulary semantic segmentation in our framework, so that the 3D panoptic segmentation is fulfilled. The project is available on https://github.com/Jun-CEN/SegmentAnyRGBD.

http://arxiv.org/abs/1411.6730v1 (2014-11-25; J. Hong, B. Lambson, S. Dhuey, J. Bokor)
In 1961, R. Landauer proposed the principle that logical irreversibility is associated with physical irreversibility and further theorized that the erasure of information is fundamentally a dissipative process. Landauer posited that a fundamental energy cost is incurred by the erasure of information contained in the memory of a computation device. His theory states that to erase one binary bit of information from a physical memory element in contact with a heat bath at a given temperature, at least kT ln(2) of heat must be dissipated from the memory into the environment, where k is the Boltzmann constant and T is the temperature. Although this connection between information theory and thermodynamics has proven to be very useful for establishing boundary limits for physical processes, Landauer principle has been a subject of some debate. Despite the theoretical controversy and fundamental importance of Landauer erasure in information technology, this phenomenon has not been experimentally explored using any practical physical implementation for digital information. ## Bekenstein, I, and the quantum of black-hole surface area
http://arxiv.org/abs/1805.03660v1 (2018-05-09; Shahar Hod)

## Disturbing the Black Hole
http://arxiv.org/abs/gr-qc/9805045v1 (1998-05-13; Jacob D. Bekenstein)
I describe some examples in support of the conjecture that the horizon area of a near equilibrium black hole is an adiabatic invariant. These include a Schwarzschild black hole perturbed by quasistatic scalar fields (which may be minimally or nonminimally coupled to curvature), a Kerr black under the influence of scalar radiation at the superradiance treshold, and a Reissner--Nordström black hole absorbing a charge marginally. These clarify somewhat the conditions under which the conjecture would be true. The desired ``adiabatic theorem'' provides an important motivation for a scheme for black hole quantization.

## Black hole polarization and new entropy bounds
http://arxiv.org/abs/gr-qc/9903002v2 (1999-02-28; Jacob D. Bekenstein, Avraham E. Mayo)
Zaslavskii has suggested how to tighten Bekenstein's bound on entropy when the object is electrically charged. Recently Hod has provided a second tighter version of the bound applicable when the object is rotating. Here we derive Zaslavskii's optimized bound by considering the accretion of an ordinary charged object by a black hole. The force originating from the polarization of the black hole by a nearby charge is central to the derivation of the bound from the generalized second law. We also conjecture an entropy bound for charged rotating objects, a synthesis of Zaslavskii's and Hod's. On the basis of the no hair principle for black holes, we show that this last bound cannot be tightened further in a generic way by knowledge of ``global'' conserved charges, e.g., baryon number, which may be borne by the object.

## The Dark Energy Survey
http://arxiv.org/abs/astro-ph/0510346v1 (2005-10-12;  The Dark Energy Survey Collaboration)
We describe the Dark Energy Survey (DES), a proposed optical-near infrared survey of 5000 sq. deg of the South Galactic Cap to ~24th magnitude in SDSS griz, that would use a new 3 sq. deg CCD camera to be mounted on the Blanco 4-m telescope at Cerro Telolo Inter-American Observatory (CTIO). The survey data will allow us to measure the dark energy and dark matter densities and the dark energy equation of state through four independent methods: galaxy clusters, weak gravitational lensing tomography, galaxy angular clustering, and supernova distances. These methods are doubly complementary: they constrain different combinations of cosmological model parameters and are subject to different systematic errors. By deriving the four sets of measurements from the same data set with a common analysis framework, we will obtain important cross checks of the systematic errors and thereby make a substantial and robust advance in the precision of dark energy measurements.

## Bremermann's limit in cGh-physics
http://arxiv.org/abs/0910.3424v4 (2009-10-18; Gennady Gorelik)
Do physical laws limit the speed of "all data processing systems, manmade as well as biological"? This question proposed and positively answered by H. J. Bremermann in 1962, should be corrected to make it compatible with Einstein's theory of gravity (aka General Relativity, or GR). As a result, the Bremermann's limit, proportional to mass M of any computer, Mc^2/h = ~ (M/gram)10^47 bits per second, should be replaced by the absolute limit (c^5/Gh)^1/2= ~ 10^43 bits per second, where the universal constants c, G, and h are the speed of light, the gravitational constant, and Planck's constant.

## Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit for an arbitrary fidelity
http://arxiv.org/abs/2301.10063v3 (2023-01-24; Niklas Hörnedal, Ole Sönnerborn)
The Mandelstam-Tamm and Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limits are two well-known evolution time estimates for isolated quantum systems. These bounds are usually formulated for fully distinguishable initial and final states, but both have tight extensions to systems that evolve between states with an arbitrary fidelity. However, the foundations of these extensions differ in some essential respects. The extended Mandelstam-Tamm quantum speed limit has been proven analytically and has a clear geometric interpretation. Furthermore, which systems saturate the limit is known. The derivation of the extended Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit, on the other hand, is based on numerical estimates. Moreover, the limit lacks a geometric interpretation, and no complete characterization of the systems reaching it exists. In this paper, we derive the extended Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit analytically and describe the systems that saturate the limit in detail. We also provide the limit with a symplectic-geometric interpretation, which indicates that it is of a different character than most existing quantum speed limits. At the end of the paper, we analyze the maximum of the extended Mande

## Note on the Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit for arbitrary fidelity
http://arxiv.org/abs/2307.16854v2 (2023-07-31; Krzysztof Andrzejewski, Katarzyna Bolonek-Lasoń, Piotr Kosiński)
For vanishing fidelity between initial and final states two important quantum speed limits, the Mandelstam-Tamm limit (involving energy dispersion) and Margolus-Levitin one (involving excitation energy expectation value) have been derived. While the generalization of the former limit to the case of arbitrary fidelity is straightforward, the relevant generalization of the latter, given in the seminal paper by Giovanetti et al (Phys. Rev. A67 (2003), 052109) was based on the conjectured equality of lower and upper bounds on the right hand side of generalized Margolus-Levitin inequality, verified numerically up to seven digits. Only recently there appear two proofs of the conjecture. We provide below a very elementary new proof, based on the simplest tools from differential calculus. Thus the generalized Margolus-Levitin speed limit can be derived much in the spirit of the original one valid for vanishing fidelity.

## A Margolus-Levitin speed limit for observables: mean energy bounds expectation-value change quadratically
http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22658v2 (2026-08-23; Bryan Nasr)
The Mandelstam-Tamm and Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limits bound how fast a state evolves, using the energy variance and the mean energy above the ground state. Speed limits on observables -- the change of an expectation value <A(t)> -- have so far used the variance (the Mandelstam-Tamm / quantum-Fisher-information lineage); the mean energy has not been brought to bear on the change of <A> in a fixed state. We close this branch. First, a no-go theorem: there is no state-independent linear mean-energy bound on the time to change an observable's expectation by Delta = |<A(T)> - <A(0)>|; the optimal state-independent exponent of Delta is exactly two. Second, the corresponding sharp quadratic bound, T (<H> - E_0) >= C_* Delta^2 / sigma_A^2, for every time-independent Hamiltonian H (ground energy E_0), every bounded observable A with spectral spread sigma_A = (lambda_max - lambda_min)/2, and every pure or mixed state, with the dimension-independent constant C_* = 1/(8 sin x_*) = 0.172506267461..., where x_* is the smallest positive root of tan(x/2) = x. The bound is tight, approached but not attained by a near-ground two-level family. We then give the exact energy-time/swing trade-of

## The underlying order induced by orthogonality and the quantum speed limit
http://arxiv.org/abs/2104.01802v2 (2021-04-05; Francisco J. Sevilla, Andrea Valdés-Hernández, Alan J. Barrios)
We perform a comprehensive analysis of the set of parameters $\{r_{i}\}$ that provide the energy distribution of pure qutrits that evolve towards a distinguishable state at a finite time $τ$, when evolving under an arbitrary and time-independent Hamiltonian. The orthogonality condition is exactly solved, revealing a non-trivial interrelation between $τ$ and the energy spectrum and allowing the classification of $\{r_{i}\}$ into families organized in a 2-simplex, $δ^{2}$. Furthermore, the states determined by $\{r_{i}\}$ are likewise analyzed according to their quantum-speed limit. Namely, we construct a map that distinguishes those $r_{i}$s in $δ^{2}$ correspondent to states whose orthogonality time is limited by the Mandelstam--Tamm bound from those restricted by the Margolus--Levitin one. Our results offer a complete characterization of the physical quantities that become relevant in both the preparation and study of the dynamics of three-level states evolving towards orthogonality.

## Systems that saturate the Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit
http://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23237v2 (2025-11-28; Ole Sönnerborn)
We provide a complete characterization of all finite-dimensional quantum systems that saturate the Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit at arbitrary Uhlmann-Jozsa fidelity. Employing a purification-based approach, we prove that mixed-state saturation occurs precisely when three structural criteria are fulfilled: the state's support is confined to the sum of two energy eigenspaces (the ground level and a single excited level); each eigenvector of the state with nonzero weight is a fixed superposition of one ground- and one excited-state energy eigenvector (determined by the minimizer of the objective function identified by Giovannetti et al.) and all such eigenvectors evolve in mutually orthogonal subspaces. These requirements impose a strict rank bound, ruling out saturation by any faithful state. For quantum bits, we derive a purity-resolved and tight Margolus-Levitin bound that reduces to the pure-state result in the limit of unit purity. Through a time-reversal argument, we further extend the dual Margolus-Levitin quantum speed limit to mixed states and establish the corresponding saturation conditions.

## Bremermann's limit
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bremermann%27s_limit
Bremermann's limit, named after Hans-Joachim Bremermann, is a theoretical limit on the maximum rate of computation that can be achieved in a self-contained system in the material universe. It is derived from Einstein's mass–energy equivalency and the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, and is c2/h ≈ 1.3563925 × 1050 bits per second per kilogram. 
This value establishes an asymptotic bound on adversarial resources when designing cryptographic algorithms, as it can be used to determine the minimum size of encryption keys or hash values required to create an algorithm that could never be cracked by a brute-force search. For example, a computer with the mass of the entire Earth operating at Bremermann's limit could perform approximately 1075 mathematical computations per second. If one assumes that a cryptographic key can be tested with only one operation, then a typical 128-bit key could be cracked in under 10−36 seconds. However, a 256-bit key (which is already in use in some systems) would take about two minutes to crack. Using a 512-bit key would increase the cracking time to approaching 1072 years, without increasing the time for encryption by more than a constant factor (depending 

## Dyson's eternal intelligence
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyson%27s_eternal_intelligence
Dyson's eternal intelligence is a theoretical framework, proposed by Freeman Dyson in his 1979 paper "Time without end: Physics and biology in an open universe,"  through which an intelligent form of life could perform an infinite number of computations, and thus experience an infinite subjective time using only a finite amount of energy. This concept relies on the life form adapting its metabolism and speed of thought to the decreasing temperature of an open, ever-expanding universe. The mathematical precision of the theory is rooted in the principles of thermodynamics, information theory, and the ultimate physical limits of computation.

## Landauer's principle
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landauer%27s_principle
Landauer's principle is a physical principle pertaining to a lower theoretical limit of energy consumption of computation. It holds that an irreversible change in information stored in a computer, such as merging two computational paths, dissipates a minimum amount of heat to its surroundings. In the formulation of Charles H. Bennett, any logically irreversible manipulation of information, such as the erasure of a bit or the merging of two computation paths, must be accompanied by a corresponding entropy increase in non-information-bearing degrees of freedom of the information-processing apparatus or its environment; the entropy thus resides in the physical hardware and its surroundings, not in the bit as a logical entity. It is hypothesized that energy consumption below this lower bound would require the development of reversible computing.
The principle was first proposed by Rolf Landauer in 1961.

## Quantum speed limit
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_speed_limit
In quantum mechanics, a quantum speed limit (QSL) is a limitation on the minimum time for a quantum system to evolve between two distinguishable (orthogonal) states. QSL theorems are closely related to time-energy uncertainty relations. In 1945, Leonid Mandelstam and Igor Tamm derived a time-energy uncertainty relation that bounds the speed of evolution in terms of the energy dispersion. Over half a century later, Norman Margolus and Lev Levitin showed that the speed of evolution cannot exceed the mean energy, a result known as the Margolus–Levitin theorem. Realistic physical systems in contact with an environment are known as open quantum systems and their evolution is also subject to QSL. Quite remarkably it was shown that environmental effects, such as non-Markovian dynamics can speed up quantum processes, which was verified in a cavity QED experiment.
QSL have been used to explore the limits of computation and complexity. In 2017, QSLs were studied in a quantum oscillator at high temperature. In 2018, it was shown that QSL are not restricted to the quantum domain and that similar bounds hold in classical systems. In 2021, both the Mandelstam-Tamm and the Margolus–Levitin QSL bo

## Transcomputational problem
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcomputational_problem
In computational complexity theory, a transcomputational problem is a problem that requires processing of more than 1093 bits of information. Any number greater than 1093 is called a transcomputational number. The number 1093, called Bremermann's limit, is, according to Hans-Joachim Bremermann, the total number of bits processed by a hypothetical computer the size of the Earth within a time period equal to the estimated age of the Earth. The term transcomputational was coined by Bremermann.

## Bekenstein bound
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bekenstein_bound
In physics, the Bekenstein bound (named after Jacob Bekenstein) is an upper limit on the thermodynamic entropy S, or Shannon entropy H, that can be contained within a given finite region of space which has a finite amount of energy—or equivalently, the maximum amount of information that is required to perfectly describe a given physical system down to the quantum level. It implies that the information of a physical system, or the information necessary to perfectly describe that system, must be finite if the region of space and the energy are finite.

## Holographic principle
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holographic_principle
The holographic principle is a property of string theories and a supposed property of quantum gravity that states that the description of a volume of space can be thought of as encoded on a lower-dimensional boundary to the region – such as a light-like boundary like a gravitational horizon. First proposed by Gerard 't Hooft in 1993, it was given a precise string theoretic interpretation by Leonard Susskind, who combined his ideas with previous ones of 't Hooft and Charles Thorn. Susskind said, "The three-dimensional world of ordinary experience—the universe filled with galaxies, stars, planets, houses, boulders, and people—is a hologram, an image of reality coded on a distant two-dimensional surface." As pointed out by Raphael Bousso, Thorn observed in 1978 that string theory admits a lower-dimensional description from  which gravity emerges in what would now be called a holographic way. The prime example of holography is the AdS/CFT correspondence.
The holographic principle was inspired by the Bekenstein bound of black hole thermodynamics, which conjectures that the maximum entropy in any region scales with the radius squared, rather than cubed as might be expected. In the case o

## Jacob Bekenstein
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Bekenstein
Jacob David Bekenstein (Hebrew: יעקב דוד בקנשטיין; May 1, 1947 – August 16, 2015) was a Mexican-born American-Israeli theoretical physicist who made fundamental contributions to the foundation of black hole thermodynamics and to other aspects of the connections between information and gravitation.

## Black hole thermodynamics
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hole_thermodynamics
In physics, black hole thermodynamics is a set of physical relationships between the properties of black holes that stands in direct relationship to classical laws of thermodynamics. The equivalence is developed by replacing entropy with black hole horizon area and replacing temperature with black hole horizon surface gravity. Having temperature implies that a black hole must emit radiation, that is, Hawking radiation.
There is no known way to verify black hole thermodynamics; it is the most widely accepted physical model that combines general relativity, quantum field theory, and thermodynamics, though Hawking's area law has already been tested by analyzing gravitational waves.

## Bousso's holographic bound
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bousso%27s_holographic_bound
The Bousso bound captures a fundamental relation between quantum information and the geometry of space and time. It appears to be an imprint of a unified theory that combines quantum mechanics with Einstein's general relativity. The study of black hole thermodynamics and the information paradox led to the idea of the holographic principle: the entropy of matter and radiation in a spatial region cannot exceed the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy of the boundary of the region, which is proportional to the boundary area. However, this "spacelike" entropy bound fails in cosmology; for example, it does not hold true in our universe. The Fischler–Susskind holographic bound was proposed to address these problems. It however failed in certain situations such as closed or collapsing cosmologies and was generalized by Bousso into the covariant entropy bound.
Raphael Bousso showed that the spacelike entropy bound is violated more broadly in many dynamical settings. For example, the entropy of a collapsing star, once inside a black hole, will eventually exceed its surface area. Due to relativistic length contraction, even ordinary thermodynamic systems can be enclosed in an arbitrarily small area.

Yokoyama, T. Yokozawa, A. Yoon, T. Yoshioka, Hang Yu, Haocun Yu, H. Yuzurihara, A. Zadrożny, M. Zanolin, S. Zeidler, T. Zelenova, J. -P. Zendri, M. Zevin, M. Zhan, H. Zhang, J. Zhang, L. Zhang, R. Zhang, T. Zhang, C. Zhao, G. Zhao, Yue Zhao, Yuhang Zhao, Z. Zhou, X. J. Zhu, Z. -H. Zhu, A. Zicoschi, M. E. Zucker, J. Zweizig, D. Antonopoulou, Z. Arzoumanian, T. Enoto, C. M. Espinoza, S. Guillot)
We present a search for continuous gravitational-wave emission due to r-modes in the pulsar PSR J0537-6910 using data from the LIGO-Virgo Collaboration observing run O3. PSR J0537-6910 is a young energetic X-ray pulsar and is the most frequent glitcher known. The inter-glitch braking index of the pulsar suggests that gravitational-wave emission due to r-mode oscillations may play an important role in the spin evolution of this pulsar. Theoretical models confirm this possibility and predict emission at a level that can be probed by ground-based detectors. In order to explore this scenario, we search for r-mode emission in the epochs between glitches by using a contemporaneous timing ephemeris obtained from NICER data. We do not detect any signals in the theoretically expected band of 86-97 Hz, and report upper limits on the amplitude of the gravitational waves. Our results improve on previous amplitude upper limits from r-modes in J0537-6910 by a factor of up to 3 and place stringent constraints on theoretical models for r-mode driven spin-down in PSR J0537-6910, especially for higher frequencies at which our results reach below the spin-down limit defined by energy conservation.

Zito, D. Zürcher, N. Zwahlen, O. Zyukova, T. Živko, D. Žontar)
This work is on the Physics of the B Factories. Part A of this book contains a brief description of the SLAC and KEK B Factories as well as their detectors, BaBar and Belle, and data taking related issues. Part B discusses tools and methods used by the experiments in order to obtain results. The results themselves can be found in Part C. Please note that version 3 on the archive is the auxiliary version of the Physics of the B Factories book. This uses the notation alpha, beta, gamma for the angles of the Unitarity Triangle. The nominal version uses the notation phi_1, phi_2 and phi_3. Please cite this work as Eur. Phys. J. C74 (2014) 3026.

Zhong, B. Zhou, N. Zhou, S. Zhou, Y. Zhou, C. G. Zhu, H. Zhu, Y. Zhu, X. A. Zhuang, V. Zhuravlov, B. Zilka, R. Zimmermann, S. Zimmermann, M. Zinna, M. Ziolkowski, R. Zitoun, L. Zivkovic, V. V. Zmouchko, G. Zobernig, A. Zoccoli, M. zur Nedden, V. Zychacek)
A detailed study is presented of the expected performance of the ATLAS detector. The reconstruction of tracks, leptons, photons, missing energy and jets is investigated, together with the performance of b-tagging and the trigger. The physics potential for a variety of interesting physics processes, within the Standard Model and beyond, is examined. The study comprises a series of notes based on simulations of the detector and physics processes, with particular emphasis given to the data expected from the first years of operation of the LHC at CERN.

H Zeng, C. Zhang, C. Zhang, D. Zhang, J. Zhang, L. Zhang, Q. Z. Zhang, R. Zhang, S. Zhang, S. L. Zhang, Y. Zhang, Z. Zhang, J. Zhao, M. Zhao, Y. Zhao, A. Zhelezov, S. Z. Zheng, X. Z. Zheng, Y. Zheng, T. Zhou, X. Zhou, V. Zhovkovska, L. Z. Zhu, X. Zhu, X. Zhu, Y. Zhu, V. Zhukov, J. Zhuo, T. Zies, D. Zuliani, X. Zuo)
Tests of fundamental symmetries, such as $C\!PT$ and Lorentz invariance, provide sensitive probes of physics beyond the standard quantum-field-theory framework. Possible violations of these symmetries are investigated through interference effects in decays of $B_s^0$ mesons to $D_s^-π^{+}$ states. The analysis uses proton-proton collision data collected with the LHCb detector in 2015-2018 at a centre-of-mass energy of $13~\mathrm{TeV}$, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of $6~\mathrm{fb}^{-1}$. The results are interpreted within the Standard Model Extension framework, yielding sensitivities to the Lorentz-violating parameter $Δa_μ$ at the ${\cal O}(10^{-14})\,\mathrm{GeV}$ level. Without assuming Lorentz-invariance violation, the $C\!PT$-violating parameter $z$ in the $B_s^0$ system is measured to be $\mathcal{R}e(z) = 0.018 \pm 0.016 \pm 0.003$ and $\mathcal{I}m(z) = 0.003 \pm 0.004 \pm 0.002$, where the first uncertainties are statistical and the second systematic. These results represent the most precise constraints on $C\!PT$ violation in the $B_s^0$ system.

Waldi, T. Likhomanenko, A. Malinin, V. Shevchenko, A. Ustyuzhanin, F. Martinez Vidal, A. Oyanguren, P. Ruiz Valls, C. Sanchez Mayordomo, C. J. G. Onderwater, H. W. Wilschut, E. Pesen)
A joint measurement is presented of the branching fractions $B^0_s\toμ^+μ^-$ and $B^0\toμ^+μ^-$ in proton-proton collisions at the LHC by the CMS and LHCb experiments. The data samples were collected in 2011 at a centre-of-mass energy of 7 TeV, and in 2012 at 8 TeV. The combined analysis produces the first observation of the $B^0_s\toμ^+μ^-$ decay, with a statistical significance exceeding six standard deviations, and the best measurement of its branching fraction so far. Furthermore, evidence for the $B^0\toμ^+μ^-$ decay is obtained with a statistical significance of three standard deviations. The branching fraction measurements are statistically compatible with SM predictions and impose stringent constraints on several theories beyond the SM.

## Bremermann's limit in cGh-physics
http://arxiv.org/abs/0910.3424v4 (2009-10-18; Gennady Gorelik)
Do physical laws limit the speed of "all data processing systems, manmade as well as biological"? This question proposed and positively answered by H. J. Bremermann in 1962, should be corrected to make it compatible with Einstein's theory of gravity (aka General Relativity, or GR). As a result, the Bremermann's limit, proportional to mass M of any computer, Mc^2/h = ~ (M/gram)10^47 bits per second, should be replaced by the absolute limit (c^5/Gh)^1/2= ~ 10^43 bits per second, where the universal constants c, G, and h are the speed of light, the gravitational constant, and Planck's constant.

## Feedback Bits Allocation for Interference Minimization in Cognitive Radio Communications
http://arxiv.org/abs/1604.01464v1 (2016-04-06; Mirza Golam Kibria, Fang Yuan, Fumihide Kojima)
This letter studies the limited feedback cognitive radio system, where the primary users (PU) are interfered by the secondary transmitter (ST) due to the imperfect beamforming. We propose to allocate the feedback bits among multiple PUs to minimize the maximum interference caused by the ST, by exploiting the heterogeneous average channel gains. In addition, we study the problem of minimizing the total feedback bits under a predefined interference threshold at the PUs. The solutions with low complexity are proposed for the studied problems, and the performances of bit allocations are analyzed. Simulation results validate our analysis and demonstrate that the proposed solutions work very well in terms of minimizing the maximum interference caused by the ST and minimizing the total feedback bits under predefined interference threshold at the PUs for limited feedback CR system.

## Massive MIMO and NOMA Bits-per-Antenna Efficiency under Power Allocation Policies
http://arxiv.org/abs/2301.03101v1 (2023-01-08; Thiago Alves Bruza Alves, Taufik Abrão)
A comparative resource allocation analysis in terms of received bits-per-antenna spectral efficiency (SE) and energy efficiency (EE) in downlink (DL) single-cell massive multiple-input multiple-output (mMIMO) and non-orthogonal multiple access (NOMA) systems considering a BS equipped with many ($M$) antennas, while $K$ devices {operate} with a single-antenna, and {the loading of devices} $ρ= \frac{K}{M}$ ranging in $0<ρ\leq 2$ is carried out under three different \ac{PA} strategies: the inverse of the channel power allocation (PICPA), a modified water-filling ($Δ$-WF) allocation method, and the equal power allocation (EPA) reference method. Since the two devices per cluster are overlapped in the power domain in the NOMA system, the channel matrix requires transformation to perform the zero-forcing (ZF) precoding adopted in mMIMO. Hence, NOMA operating under many antennas can favor a group of devices with higher array gain, overcoming the mMIMO and operating conveniently in the higher loading range $0.6<ρ<2.0$. In such a scenario, a more realistic and helpful metric consists of {evaluating} the area under SE and EE curves, by measuring the bit-per-antenna and bit-per-antenna-per-wat

## A Better Definition of the Kilogram
http://arxiv.org/abs/0709.2576v1 (2007-09-17; Ronald F. Fox, Theodore P. Hill)
Fixing the value of Avogadro's constant, the number of atoms in 12 grams of carbon-12, at exactly 84446886^3 would imply that one gram is the mass of exactly 18x14074481^3 carbon-12 atoms. This new definition of the gram, and thereby also the kilogram, is precise, elegant and unchanging in time, unlike the current 118-year-old artifact kilogram in Paris and the proposed experimental definitions of the kilogram using man-made silicon spheres or the watt balance apparatus.

## Interstellar Communication. VIII. Hard limits on the number of bits per photon
http://arxiv.org/abs/1801.06218v1 (2018-01-18; Michael Hippke)
A photon can encode several bits of information based on an alphabet of its time of arrival, energy, and polarization. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle places a limit on measuring pairs of physical properties of a particle, limiting the maximal information efficiency to <59 bits per photon in practice, and <171 bits per photon at Planck energy, at a data rate of one photon per second.

## Landauer Principle and General Relativity
http://arxiv.org/abs/2003.07436v1 (2020-03-16; L. Herrera)
We endeavour to illustrate the physical relevance of the Landauer principle applying it to different important issues concerning the theory of gravitation. We shall first analyze, in the context of general relativity, the consequences derived from the fact, implied by Landauer principle, that information has mass. Next, we shall analyze the role played by the Landauer principle in order to understand why different congruences of observers provide very different physical descriptions of the same space--time. Finally, we shall apply the Landauer principle to the problem of gravitational radiation. We shall see that the fact that gravitational radiation is an irreversible process entailing dissipation, is a straightforward consequence of the Landauer principle and of the fact that gravitational radiation conveys information. An expression measuring the part of radiated energy that corresponds to the radiated information and an expression defining the total number of bits erased in that process, shall be obtained, as well as an explicit expression linking the latter to the Bondi news function.

## Modified Landauer principle according to Tsallis entropy
http://arxiv.org/abs/2411.07897v1 (2024-11-12; L. Herrera)
The Landauer principle establishes a lower bound in the amount of energy that should be dissipated in the erasure of one bit of information. The specific value of this dissipated energy is tightly related to the definition of entropy. In this article, we present a generalization of the Landauer principle based on the Tsallis entropy. Some consequences resulting from such a generalization are discussed. These consequences include the modification to the mass ascribed to one bit of information, the generalization of the Landauer principle to the case when the system is embedded in a gravitational field, and the number of bits radiated in the emission of gravitational waves.

## Information and thermodynamics: Experimental verification of Landauer's erasure principle
http://arxiv.org/abs/1503.06537v1 (2015-03-23; Antoine Bérut, Artyom Petrosyan, Sergio Ciliberto)
We present an experiment in which a one-bit memory is constructed, using a system of a single colloidal particle trapped in a modulated double-well potential. We measure the amount of heat dissipated to erase a bit and we establish that in the limit of long erasure cycles the mean dissipated heat saturates at the Landauer bound, i.e. the minimal quantity of heat necessarily produced to delete a classical bit of information. This result demonstrates the intimate link between information theory and thermodynamics. To stress this connection we also show that a detailed Jarzynski equality is verified, retrieving the Landauer's bound independently of the work done on the system. The experimental details are presented and the experimental errors carefully discussed

## Hard Probes 2012: Experimental Summary
http://arxiv.org/abs/1210.3975v2 (2012-10-15; J. Schukraft)
The 5th international Conference on Hard and Electromagnetic Probes in High-Energy Nuclear Collisions was held in May 2012 in Cagliari, Italy. This contribution summarises some of the experimental highlights presented at the meeting, concentrating on new results from LHC and RHIC on parton energy loss ('jet-quenching') and heavy quark meson production ('quarkonia suppression').

## World Summary of $α_s$ (2012)
http://arxiv.org/abs/1210.0325v1 (2012-10-01; Siegfried Bethke)
Determinations of the strong coupling strength, $α_s$, are summarised and a new world average value of $α_s (M_Z)$ is determined, using a new method of pre-averaging results within classes of measurements like hadronic $τ$ decays, deep inelastic scattering processes, lattice calculations, electron-positron annihilation processes and electro-weak precision fits. The overall result is $$α_s (M_Z) = 0.1184 \pm 0.0007,$$ unchanged from the value obtained in 2009. This presentation is an excerpt from the QCD review section of the 2012 Review of Particle Physics (RPP) of the Particle Data Group.
